### Benchmarking Reasoning Robustness in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04550v1)
- **Authors**: Tong Yu, Yongcheng Jing, Xikun Zhang, Wentao Jiang, Wenjie Wu, Yingjie Wang, Wenbin Hu, Bo Du, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: Despite the recent success of large language models (LLMs) in reasoning such as DeepSeek, we for the first time identify a key dilemma in reasoning robustness and generalization: significant performance degradation on novel or incomplete data, suggesting a reliance on memorized patterns rather than systematic reasoning. Our closer examination reveals four key unique limitations underlying this issue:(1) Positional bias--models favor earlier queries in multi-query inputs but answering the wrong one in the latter (e.g., GPT-4o's accuracy drops from 75.8 percent to 72.8 percent); (2) Instruction sensitivity--performance declines by 5.0 to 7.5 percent in the Qwen2.5 Series and by 5.0 percent in DeepSeek-V3 with auxiliary guidance; (3) Numerical fragility--value substitution sharply reduces accuracy (e.g., GPT-4o drops from 97.5 percent to 82.5 percent, GPT-o1-mini drops from 97.5 percent to 92.5 percent); and (4) Memory dependence--models resort to guesswork when missing critical data. These findings further highlight the reliance on heuristic recall over rigorous logical inference, demonstrating challenges in reasoning robustness. To comprehensively investigate these robustness challenges, this paper introduces a novel benchmark, termed as Math-RoB, that exploits hallucinations triggered by missing information to expose reasoning gaps. This is achieved by an instruction-based approach to generate diverse datasets that closely resemble training distributions, facilitating a holistic robustness assessment and advancing the development of more robust reasoning frameworks. Bad character(s) in field Abstract.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Math-RoB, a new benchmark designed to assess the reasoning robustness of large language models (LLMs) in mathematical tasks. The authors identify four key robustness issues: positional bias (preferring earlier queries), instruction sensitivity (declines with auxiliary instructions), numerical fragility (sensitivity to value changes), and memory dependence (reliance on pre-trained patterns for missing data). Math-RoB includes datasets tailored to expose these weaknesses, using techniques like increasing text length, substituting definitions, modifying numerical values, and deleting key information. The authors evaluate a range of open- and closed-source LLMs, finding significant performance degradation on novel or incomplete data, suggesting a reliance on memorized patterns rather than genuine reasoning. They also propose a Memory Completion Rate (MCR) metric to quantify the filling-in of missing information.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic approach to identifying and testing specific robustness issues in LLM reasoning, as opposed to simply measuring overall accuracy. The design of the Math-RoB benchmark, with its targeted datasets, is a significant contribution. The MCR metric is also a novel attempt to quantify a specific type of failure mode. However, some of the identified weaknesses (e.g., instruction sensitivity) are not entirely new observations in the field. Prior work has hinted at these weaknesses.

**Significance:** The paper's significance is moderate. It provides a valuable tool (Math-RoB) for evaluating and comparing LLMs in terms of reasoning robustness. The identified weaknesses and the associated datasets could guide the development of more robust LLMs. By highlighting the limitations of current LLMs in handling novel or incomplete information, the paper contributes to a more nuanced understanding of their reasoning capabilities. The paper also identifies the lack of interpretability (model hallucination) which is a great point.
The main weakness is the limited scope of models evaluated. The paper emphasizes publicly available datasets. However, given the rate of progress within the closed-source community in robustness and model quality, this limits the impact of the paper. The dataset also does not cover much beyond the domain of mathematics.

**Justification for the Score:**

I assign a score of **6**.

**Strengths:**

*   Systematic approach to identifying and testing robustness issues.
*   Novel Math-RoB benchmark with targeted datasets.
*   Introduction of the MCR metric.
*   Clear demonstration of limitations in current LLM reasoning.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Some identified weaknesses have been hinted at in prior work, reducing the degree of the paper's novelty.
*   The evaluation of the data is limited to only a couple open- and closed-source systems. The authors mentioned in the limitations section the need to improve model scalability due to hardware limitations.
*   The dataset is only for mathematical reasoning. There could be other domains with this lack of robustness.

The paper provides valuable insights and a useful benchmark for evaluating reasoning robustness in LLMs, but its impact is limited by only focusing on mathematical domains, as well as the evaluation limited to public datasets.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Compositional Translation: A Novel LLM-based Approach for Low-resource Machine Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04554v1)
- **Authors**: Armel Zebaze, Benoît Sagot, Rachel Bawden
- **Abstract**: The ability of generative large language models (LLMs) to perform in-context learning has given rise to a large body of research into how best to prompt models for various natural language processing tasks. Machine Translation (MT) has been shown to benefit from in-context examples, in particular when they are semantically similar to the sentence to translate. In this paper, we propose a new LLM-based translation paradigm, compositional translation, to replace naive few-shot MT with similarity-based demonstrations. An LLM is used to decompose a sentence into simpler phrases, and then to translate each phrase with the help of retrieved demonstrations. Finally, the LLM is prompted to translate the initial sentence with the help of the self-generated phrase-translation pairs. Our intuition is that this approach should improve translation because these shorter phrases should be intrinsically easier to translate and easier to match with relevant examples. This is especially beneficial in low-resource scenarios, and more generally whenever the selection pool is small or out of domain. We show that compositional translation boosts LLM translation performance on a wide range of popular MT benchmarks, including FLORES 200, NTREX 128 and TICO-19. Code and outputs are available at https://github.com/ArmelRandy/compositional-translation
- **Summary**: Okay, I can provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper based on the provided text.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Compositional Translation" (CompTra), a novel LLM-based approach for low-resource machine translation (MT). CompTra addresses the challenge of translating into low-resource languages by decomposing a sentence into simpler phrases, translating each phrase using retrieved in-context examples, and then using the LLM to translate the initial sentence incorporating the translated phrases. The core intuition is that shorter phrases are easier to translate and to find relevant demonstrations for.  The paper demonstrates that CompTra consistently outperforms traditional few-shot MT approaches across a range of popular MT benchmarks (FLORES 200, NTREX 128, and TICO-19), as well as some existing advanced MT prompting techniques (SBYS, TEaR and MAPS). The paper also performs several ablation studies to test the importance of various choices.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the idea of leveraging compositionality in MT isn't entirely new (as the paper acknowledges), CompTra's implementation using LLMs and its specific approach to decomposition, demonstration retrieval, and recombination does present a novel and practical method. The innovation lies in the clever combination of existing ideas, and the paper does well to highlight related work in this regard. Furthermore, applying LLMs to translation by decomposing sentence into short-phrases is a very recent trend.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its potential to improve MT performance for low-resource languages. Given the limitations of LLMs in handling LRLs directly and the scarcity of data for these languages, CompTra offers a promising path forward. The consistent improvement across multiple benchmarks is compelling and the ablations provide a good analysis of the approach.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong empirical results:** The consistent improvements across different datasets and language pairs are a significant strength. The paper supports the claim that compTra enhances translation in low-resource settings.
    *   **Clear methodology:** The steps of CompTra are clearly explained, making it easy to understand and potentially reproduce.
    *   **Thorough ablation studies:** The ablation studies are valuable in understanding the contribution of each component and guiding future research.
    *   **Comparison to relevant baselines:** The comparisons against few-shot MT with similarity search and other state-of-the-art techniques strengthens the paper's findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** While leveraging LLMs is a strength, it also makes the approach dependent on the capabilities and biases of the underlying LLM. The paper doesn't deeply explore potential biases or limitations in the LLM's phrase decomposition or recombination abilities, which can vary with different LLMs. It mentions high resource directions but doesn't address why they cannot achieve the similar improvements.
    *   **Scalability Concerns:** While the use of compositionality to perform MT will help to scale translations to more languages, there is little mention how this paradigm could scale to a greater amount of different languages or domains.
    *   **Limited analysis of failure cases:** Although the paper mentions ensembling with a QE metric and notes that there are occasional disagreements with MetricX QE and there is no in-depth analysis of specific failure modes of CompTra.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in low-resource MT by encouraging the exploration of compositional methods and LLM prompting strategies. It also opens up avenues for investigating more sophisticated decomposition techniques and leveraging external knowledge sources to improve translation quality.
*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The decision to set this score was based on the thoroughness of results that the approach yields for low-resource translation. Furthermore, ablations have been performed thoroughly to understand better what is working within the approach. The primary challenge that prevents this score from being higher is that it depends heavily on LLMs and there is limited attention on other languages than English as well as a smaller analysis on failure cases.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### HybridNorm: Towards Stable and Efficient Transformer Training via Hybrid Normalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04598v1)
- **Authors**: Zhijian Zhuo, Yutao Zeng, Ya Wang, Sijun Zhang, Jian Yang, Xiaoqing Li, Xun Zhou, Jinwen Ma
- **Abstract**: Transformers have become the de facto architecture for a wide range of machine learning tasks, particularly in large language models (LLMs). Despite their remarkable performance, challenges remain in training deep transformer networks, especially regarding the location of layer normalization. While Pre-Norm structures facilitate easier training due to their more prominent identity path, they often yield suboptimal performance compared to Post-Norm. In this paper, we propose $\textbf{HybridNorm}$, a straightforward yet effective hybrid normalization strategy that integrates the advantages of both Pre-Norm and Post-Norm approaches. Specifically, HybridNorm employs QKV normalization within the attention mechanism and Post-Norm in the feed-forward network (FFN) of each transformer block. This design not only stabilizes training but also enhances performance, particularly in the context of LLMs. Comprehensive experiments in both dense and sparse architectures show that HybridNorm consistently outperforms both Pre-Norm and Post-Norm approaches, achieving state-of-the-art results across various benchmarks. These findings highlight the potential of HybridNorm as a more stable and effective technique for improving the training and performance of deep transformer models. %Code will be made publicly available. Code is available at https://github.com/BryceZhuo/HybridNorm.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HybridNorm, a novel normalization strategy for Transformers that aims to combine the benefits of both Pre-Norm and Post-Norm approaches. HybridNorm applies QKV normalization within the attention mechanism and Post-Norm in the feed-forward network (FFN) of each transformer block. The authors hypothesize that this hybrid approach stabilizes training, improves performance, and is particularly effective for large language models (LLMs). They conduct experiments on both dense and sparse (MoE) transformer architectures, demonstrating that HybridNorm outperforms Pre-Norm and Post-Norm baselines across various benchmarks. The paper includes theoretical analysis supporting the method's stability properties and empirical evidence validating its effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of combining normalization strategies isn't entirely novel. Previous work, like Mix-LN, has explored mixing Pre-Norm and Post-Norm at different layers of the network.  However, applying QKV-Norm with post-norm specifically within each transformer block is a distinct configuration. The novelty lies in the specific *combination* and its demonstrated effectiveness, but the individual components have been investigated before.

*   **Significance:** The significance hinges on the consistent empirical improvements demonstrated by the authors, particularly in the context of LLMs. If HybridNorm truly leads to more stable training and better downstream performance on large models, it represents a valuable practical contribution. However, several aspects need critical consideration:

    *   **Scope of Improvement:** The gains, while consistent, aren't always dramatic.  A critical assessment requires understanding the computational cost of implementing HybridNorm relative to the performance gains.  It is also not clearly communicated how significant it is relative to more modern LLMs.

    *   **Generalizability:** While the paper presents results on several benchmarks, it's essential to see how well HybridNorm generalizes to even larger models and different datasets than those examined. LLMs are also extremely sensitive to hyperparameter selection, so the benefit may be overstated on the selected hyperparameters.

    *   **Theoretical Depth:** The theoretical analysis, while present, is somewhat limited. A deeper theoretical understanding of *why* this particular combination works better than others would significantly enhance the paper's contribution. This is further weakened if the LLMs are using architectures different than the paper uses.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-written presentation.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation covering both dense and sparse models.
    *   Theoretical analysis provides some justification for the observed empirical improvements.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Incremental novelty: Builds upon existing ideas of combining normalization strategies.
    *   The magnitude of the empirical improvements might be overstated. It would also improve if more modern baselines and LLMs are used.
    *   Limited theoretical depth: Lacks a fully satisfying explanation of *why* HybridNorm works better.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the above analysis, I assign a score of **6**. The paper makes a meaningful contribution by demonstrating a practical and effective normalization strategy. However, the incremental nature of the novelty and the limited theoretical depth prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. The empirical results are compelling, but the gains aren't overwhelmingly large and warrant further investigation regarding generalizability to even larger, more diverse models.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### The Best of Both Worlds: Integrating Language Models and Diffusion Models for Video Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04606v1)
- **Authors**: Aoxiong Yin, Kai Shen, Yichong Leng, Xu Tan, Xinyu Zhou, Juncheng Li, Siliang Tang
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in text-to-video (T2V) generation have been driven by two competing paradigms: autoregressive language models and diffusion models. However, each paradigm has intrinsic limitations: language models struggle with visual quality and error accumulation, while diffusion models lack semantic understanding and causal modeling. In this work, we propose LanDiff, a hybrid framework that synergizes the strengths of both paradigms through coarse-to-fine generation. Our architecture introduces three key innovations: (1) a semantic tokenizer that compresses 3D visual features into compact 1D discrete representations through efficient semantic compression, achieving a $\sim$14,000$\times$ compression ratio; (2) a language model that generates semantic tokens with high-level semantic relationships; (3) a streaming diffusion model that refines coarse semantics into high-fidelity videos. Experiments show that LanDiff, a 5B model, achieves a score of 85.43 on the VBench T2V benchmark, surpassing the state-of-the-art open-source models Hunyuan Video (13B) and other commercial models such as Sora, Keling, and Hailuo. Furthermore, our model also achieves state-of-the-art performance in long video generation, surpassing other open-source models in this field. Our demo can be viewed at https://landiff.github.io/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "The Best of Both Worlds: Integrating Language Models and Diffusion Models for Video Generation" proposes a novel text-to-video (T2V) generation framework called LanDiff. LanDiff aims to combine the strengths of autoregressive language models (LLMs) and diffusion models while mitigating their respective limitations. It achieves this through a coarse-to-fine generation paradigm. Key innovations include: (1) a semantic tokenizer that compresses 3D visual features into compact 1D discrete representations with high compression; (2) an LLM for generating semantic tokens with high-level semantic relationships; and (3) a streaming diffusion model to refine coarse semantics into high-fidelity videos.  The authors demonstrate through experiments that LanDiff outperforms existing open-source T2V models (including Hunyuan Video) and even compares favorably with closed-source commercial models on the VBench benchmark. The paper also reports state-of-the-art performance in long video generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of combining LLMs for semantic understanding and diffusion models for high-fidelity rendering is not entirely new.  Hybrid approaches have been explored in image generation and some earlier video generation attempts. However, LanDiff's specific architecture, particularly the design of its semantic tokenizer (achieving extremely high compression rates) and the streaming diffusion model for long videos, displays significant innovation. The architecture's commitment to a carefully-designed two-stage process and division of labor between LM and diffusion is thoughtfully executed.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in T2V generation: balancing semantic coherence with visual quality. By demonstrating superior performance on a standardized benchmark like VBench, LanDiff makes a noticeable advancement.  The reported improvements in long video generation are also practically significant, as many current systems struggle with temporal consistency over extended durations.  The very high semantic compression with the tokenizer is potentially a powerful ingredient for future work in this area.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The quantitative results on VBench, outperforming both open-source and commercial models (according to the authors), are compelling. The qualitative examples further showcase LanDiff's ability to generate semantically relevant and visually appealing videos.
    *   **Well-Defined Architecture:** The components of LanDiff are clearly described, and the rationale for each design choice is well explained. The paper focuses on the role for *each* module: LM and Diffusion.
    *   **Addressing Key Limitations:** The framework strategically tackles limitations of both LLMs and diffusion models, such as error accumulation in autoregressive models and semantic inconsistencies in diffusion models.
    *   **High Semantic Compression:** One of the strengths is its extreme semantic compression (14,000x), a technical ingredient that is very useful.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on Pre-trained Models:** LanDiff relies on pre-trained models like Theia and builds upon existing diffusion model architectures. While leveraging pre-trained components is common, it reduces the overall novelty of the system. The authors themselves didn't design those components *per se*.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While the paper includes some ablation studies, further analysis of the individual components' contributions (especially the LLM and Diffusion model configuration) would strengthen the claims. Specifically, it's somewhat challenging to disentangle exactly *why* LanDiff outperforms, other than the architectural design.
    *   **Reproducibility Concerns:** Lack of code release, even at the time of writing, is a substantial weakness.  Although the authors provide architecture details, reproducing the results without access to the specific implementation and training data could be difficult.
    *   **Benchmark Integrity:** The paper uses VBench as an evaluation tool, but it relies upon self-reported scores from competitors. Further more nuanced analysis would be very interesting: analyzing when LanDiff works, when it doesn't, and *why*.

*   **Potential Influence:** LanDiff's architecture and results are likely to influence future research in T2V generation. The semantic tokenizer design is a valuable contribution, and the integration strategy provides a blueprint for combining different generative modeling paradigms. The impact will largely hinge on the open-sourcing of the code to encourage greater accessibility and downstream innovation.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:** LanDiff makes a significant contribution to the T2V generation field by demonstrating a strong hybrid approach.  The specific design choices, the semantic tokenizer, and the strong empirical results (outperforming commercial systems by the authors' account) represent real advancement.  However, the reliance on pre-trained models, the limited scope of ablation studies, and most significantly, the lack of code release, reduce the potential for impact and further research. While not a groundbreaking paradigm shift, it is a well-executed engineering effort and the novel technical contribution for semantic compression with tokenizer is an important ingredient.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Data-Efficient Language Models: A Child-Inspired Approach to Language Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04611v1)
- **Authors**: Mohammad Amin Ghanizadeh, Mohammad Javad Dousti
- **Abstract**: In this work, we explain our approach employed in the BabyLM Challenge, which uses various methods of training language models (LMs) with significantly less data compared to traditional large language models (LLMs) and are inspired by how human children learn. While a human child is exposed to far less linguistic input than an LLM, they still achieve remarkable language understanding and generation abilities. To this end, we develop a model trained on a curated dataset consisting of 10 million words, primarily sourced from child-directed transcripts. The 2024 BabyLM Challenge initial dataset of 10M words is filtered to 8.5M. Next, it is supplemented with a randomly selected subset of TVR dataset consisting of 1.5M words of television dialogues. The latter dataset ensures that similar to children, the model is also exposed to language through media. Furthermore, we reduce the vocabulary size to 32,000 tokens, aligning it with the limited vocabulary of children in the early stages of language acquisition. We use curriculum learning and is able to match the baseline on certain benchmarks while surpassing the baseline on others. Additionally, incorporating common LLM training datasets, such as MADLAD-400, degrades performance. These findings underscore the importance of dataset selection, vocabulary scaling, and curriculum learning in creating more data-efficient language models that better mimic human learning processes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents an approach to training data-efficient language models (LMs), inspired by how children learn language. The key ideas involve: 1) Curating a dataset primarily from child-directed transcripts, supplemented with TV dialogue data. 2) Scaling down the vocabulary size to mimic the limited vocabulary of children during early language acquisition. 3) Implementing curriculum learning to gradually expose the model to more complex linguistic inputs. The paper explores the impact of these techniques on benchmark performance and reports that a combination of dataset curation, vocabulary scaling, and curriculum learning can yield competitive results compared to baselines, while using significantly less data than traditional large language models. The paper also investigates the impact of adding MADLAD-400 dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** While the idea of drawing inspiration from child language acquisition for training LMs isn't entirely new, the paper makes some incremental contributions. The specific combination of dataset curation (focusing on child-directed speech and including TV data), vocabulary scaling, and curriculum learning, combined with an analysis of its effects is the primary novel contribution. The exploration of MADLAD-400, while resulting in negative findings, is useful to the community as it cautions against simply using any large dataset for data efficient training. The curriculum learning scoring functions are described, but these are not entirely new. The novelty is in their application to this specific challenge.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in demonstrating that data-efficient LMs are possible through careful design and training methodologies.  Successfully reducing the data requirements without significant performance degradation is crucial for resource-constrained settings and for furthering our understanding of learning in both machines and humans. However, the reported improvements are incremental and it's difficult to judge the *real* downstream impact of such a model. The discussion of data valuation and attribution methods, while insightful, isn't a core part of the implemented work, which slightly diminishes the paper's impact. There is limited detailed error analysis. The experiments could have included deeper analyses of the types of errors the model makes and how those errors correlate with the training data choices.

* **Strengths:**
    * Clear and well-structured presentation.
    * Empirical evaluation of key design choices.
    *  Interesting findings regarding vocabulary size and curriculum learning.
    * A good starting point for future work.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The improvements compared to the baseline are not dramatically large.
    * The choice of SmolLM is a bit limiting. An analysis of whether this approach transfers to *other* model architectures (especially larger ones) would strengthen the results.
    *  Lack of rigorous error analysis.
    *  Limited exploration of other curriculum learning strategies (e.g., different scoring functions, different ordering of data points).
    * The paper could more deeply connect its findings with theories of child language acquisition. What specific aspects of human learning are being effectively captured? This connection would make the work more compelling for cognitive scientists.

* **Overall:** The paper offers a valuable, albeit incremental, contribution to the field of data-efficient language modeling. While the results are not groundbreaking, the paper provides useful insights into dataset curation, vocabulary scaling, and curriculum learning, demonstrating that careful choices in these areas can lead to improved performance with limited data. It lays a foundation for future research exploring more sophisticated data valuation and curriculum learning techniques.

Score: 6.  The paper provides valuable insights and a combination of useful techniques, but the incremental nature of the results and limitations prevent a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### START: Self-taught Reasoner with Tools
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04625v2)
- **Authors**: Chengpeng Li, Mingfeng Xue, Zhenru Zhang, Jiaxi Yang, Beichen Zhang, Xiang Wang, Bowen Yu, Binyuan Hui, Junyang Lin, Dayiheng Liu
- **Abstract**: Large reasoning models (LRMs) like OpenAI-o1 and DeepSeek-R1 have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in complex reasoning tasks through the utilization of long Chain-of-thought (CoT). However, these models often suffer from hallucinations and inefficiencies due to their reliance solely on internal reasoning processes. In this paper, we introduce START (Self-Taught Reasoner with Tools), a novel tool-integrated long CoT reasoning LLM that significantly enhances reasoning capabilities by leveraging external tools. Through code execution, START is capable of performing complex computations, self-checking, exploring diverse methods, and self-debugging, thereby addressing the limitations of LRMs. The core innovation of START lies in its self-learning framework, which comprises two key techniques: 1) Hint-infer: We demonstrate that inserting artificially designed hints (e.g., ``Wait, maybe using Python here is a good idea.'') during the inference process of a LRM effectively stimulates its ability to utilize external tools without the need for any demonstration data. Hint-infer can also serve as a simple and effective sequential test-time scaling method; 2) Hint Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (Hint-RFT): Hint-RFT combines Hint-infer and RFT by scoring, filtering, and modifying the reasoning trajectories with tool invocation generated by a LRM via Hint-infer, followed by fine-tuning the LRM. Through this framework, we have fine-tuned the QwQ-32B model to achieve START. On PhD-level science QA (GPQA), competition-level math benchmarks (AMC23, AIME24, AIME25), and the competition-level code benchmark (LiveCodeBench), START achieves accuracy rates of 63.6%, 95.0%, 66.7%, 47.1%, and 47.3%, respectively. It significantly outperforms the base QwQ-32B and achieves performance comparable to the state-of-the-art open-weight model R1-Distill-Qwen-32B and the proprietary model o1-Preview.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "START: Self-taught Reasoner with Tools":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces START, a novel tool-integrated large language model (LLM) designed to enhance reasoning capabilities by leveraging external tools, specifically a Python interpreter. START aims to address the limitations of LLMs that rely solely on internal reasoning and often struggle with complex computations or simulations.  The key innovations are: 1) a "Hint-infer" technique, which inserts designed hints into the LLM's reasoning process to stimulate tool usage without demonstration data, and 2) "Hint Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (Hint-RFT)," a self-learning framework combining Hint-infer and Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning to improve tool invocation capabilities.  The authors fine-tune the QwQ-32B-Preview model using their approach, achieving significant improvements on PhD-level science QA (GPQA), competition-level math benchmarks (AMC23, AIME24, AIME25), and a code benchmark (LiveCodeBench), outperforming the base model and approaching state-of-the-art performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents a reasonably novel approach. While tool integration in LLMs is not entirely new (e.g., Toolformer), the "Hint-infer" and "Hint-RFT" techniques are a creative way to induce and improve tool usage without extensive demonstration data. Hint-infer, in particular, is a surprisingly effective way to trigger a latent tool-using ability in LLMs. The combination of these techniques into a self-learning framework is a valuable contribution. The sequential test-time scaling effect from adding hints before the stop token is also an interesting observation.

*   **Significance:** The results are significant, demonstrating substantial improvements in performance on several challenging reasoning benchmarks. The ability to achieve near state-of-the-art performance on tasks like GPQA and competition-level math problems using a relatively simple framework is noteworthy. The open-sourcing of START also contributes to the field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The "Hint-infer" method is simple and effective.
    *   "Hint-RFT" provides a practical self-training framework for tool-integrated reasoning.
    *   The paper clearly presents the methodology and experimental setup.
    *   The performance improvements on challenging benchmarks are well-documented.
    *   The ablation study isolating the impact of tool invocation further strengthens the findings.
    *   The analysis comparing QwQ-32B-Preview with Hint-infer and START provides valuable insights.
    *   The paper is well-written and generally easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The manual design of hints, while effective, raises questions about generalizability and scalability. Developing more automated or adaptive hint generation techniques would be beneficial. The reliance on specific conjunctions for hint insertion also seems somewhat ad-hoc.
    *   The specific set of tasks may not fully represent the breadth of real-world reasoning problems.
    *   Some analysis (e.g. what kind of hint improves the performance ) could be enhanced.
    *   The success is built upon the existing QwQ-32B model, but there are limited attempts to extend to other models.

*   **Potential Influence:** START has the potential to influence the development of more capable and efficient LLMs by demonstrating the value of tool integration and self-learning techniques. The Hint-infer approach could inspire new methods for leveraging latent capabilities in LLMs without extensive data annotation. The framework can be applied to other tool-integrated systems.

*   **Score Justification:** I assign a score of 7. While tool-integration is not a completely novel idea, the "Hint-infer" and "Hint-RFT" techniques are significant contributions to *how* LLMs can learn to effectively use tools *without extensive human supervision*. The performance gains are real, and the ablation studies provide evidence that START's success is not merely due to increased training data.  The limitations regarding hint design and task diversity temper the score slightly. It is not state of the art, but it is an open-source and effective long CoT+tools reasoning model.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mark Your LLM: Detecting the Misuse of Open-Source Large Language Models via Watermarking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04636v1)
- **Authors**: Yijie Xu, Aiwei Liu, Xuming Hu, Lijie Wen, Hui Xiong
- **Abstract**: As open-source large language models (LLMs) like Llama3 become more capable, it is crucial to develop watermarking techniques to detect their potential misuse. Existing watermarking methods either add watermarks during LLM inference, which is unsuitable for open-source LLMs, or primarily target classification LLMs rather than recent generative LLMs. Adapting these watermarks to open-source LLMs for misuse detection remains an open challenge. This work defines two misuse scenarios for open-source LLMs: intellectual property (IP) violation and LLM Usage Violation. Then, we explore the application of inference-time watermark distillation and backdoor watermarking in these contexts. We propose comprehensive evaluation methods to assess the impact of various real-world further fine-tuning scenarios on watermarks and the effect of these watermarks on LLM performance. Our experiments reveal that backdoor watermarking could effectively detect IP Violation, while inference-time watermark distillation is applicable in both scenarios but less robust to further fine-tuning and has a more significant impact on LLM performance compared to backdoor watermarking. Exploring more advanced watermarking methods for open-source LLMs to detect their misuse should be an important future direction.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the critical challenge of detecting the misuse of open-source Large Language Models (LLMs) like Llama3, focusing on intellectual property (IP) violation and LLM usage violation scenarios. Recognizing the limitations of existing inference-time watermarking techniques for open-source LLMs (due to easy removal), the authors explore the application of backdoor watermarking and inference-time watermark distillation. They propose comprehensive evaluation methods to assess the robustness of these watermarks against real-world fine-tuning scenarios and their impact on LLM performance (reasoning, understanding, generation).  The study reveals that backdoor watermarking is more effective for detecting IP violations, while inference-time watermark distillation can be applied in both scenarios, albeit with less robustness and a greater impact on LLM performance.  The paper concludes by emphasizing the need for more advanced and robust watermarking methods specifically tailored for open-source LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** While the idea of watermarking LLMs is not new, the specific focus on **open-source LLMs** and the explicit definition and exploration of **two distinct misuse scenarios (IP and Usage Violation)** adds novelty. Existing watermarking methods were mostly designed for closed-source, API-driven models. Adapting backdoor and distillation techniques, which are known but usually used for other tasks, for misuse detection in *open-source* generative LLMs has merit. However, the adaptation of existing techniques is incremental. The main contribution lies in the application and evaluation rather than the invention of entirely new methods.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a highly relevant and important problem. As open-source LLMs become increasingly powerful and accessible, the risk of misuse increases.  Detecting IP violations and preventing the generation of harmful content are crucial for responsible AI development. The systematic evaluation of watermarking techniques under various fine-tuning scenarios is valuable. The study identifies the limitations of existing techniques and highlights the need for further research. These findings could inform the development of more practical and robust watermarking methods for open-source LLMs. The practical focus and the systematic evaluation of robustness against fine-tuning are significant.

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem Definition:** Clear articulation of the misuse scenarios specific to open-source LLMs.
*   **Systematic Evaluation:** Comprehensive evaluation of robustness against different fine-tuning techniques (full-parameter, LoRA, Continual Pretraining, IT, DPO, RLHF) and impact on LLM performance.
*   **Practical Focus:** Addresses a practical problem with clear real-world implications.
*   **Comprehensive Reporting:** Detailed description of experimental setup, metrics, and results. Provides code or is open to sharing

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** The core watermarking techniques (backdoor and distillation) are adaptations of existing methods rather than fundamentally new approaches.
*   **Limited Success:** The results show that neither of the explored methods provides a complete solution. They all present performance and robustness shortcomings.  This limits the immediate practical applicability of the findings.
*   **Trigger Dependence:** Backdoor methods depend on specific triggers, which might be circumvented by malicious users. This is a well-known issue with backdoor techniques.
*   **Performance Trade-off:** Distillation methods impact LLM performance, making them less desirable in some cases.
*   **Lack of comparative benchmarks to the cutting-edge:** It does not evaluate against some of the best adversarial robustness measures for watermarking models

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by focusing on the under-explored area of watermarking for *open-source* LLMs in the context of misuse detection. While the novelty of the core watermarking techniques is incremental, the systematic evaluation of robustness against fine-tuning and the explicit problem framing make the paper useful and potentially impactful. The clear articulation of limitations and the identification of future research directions further strengthen the paper. However, the limited success of the explored methods in fully addressing the problem and the dependence on existing techniques prevents a higher score. A slightly more in-depth analysis of how the *combination* of distillation and backdoor (a hybrid approach) might perform would have bolstered the conclusions.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Implicit Cross-Lingual Rewarding for Efficient Multilingual Preference Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04647v1)
- **Authors**: Wen Yang, Junhong Wu, Chen Wang, Chengqing Zong, Jiajun Zhang
- **Abstract**: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) has become a prominent method for aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) with human preferences. While DPO has enabled significant progress in aligning English LLMs, multilingual preference alignment is hampered by data scarcity. To address this, we propose a novel approach that $\textit{captures}$ learned preferences from well-aligned English models by implicit rewards and $\textit{transfers}$ them to other languages through iterative training. Specifically, we derive an implicit reward model from the logits of an English DPO-aligned model and its corresponding reference model. This reward model is then leveraged to annotate preference relations in cross-lingual instruction-following pairs, using English instructions to evaluate multilingual responses. The annotated data is subsequently used for multilingual DPO fine-tuning, facilitating preference knowledge transfer from English to other languages. Fine-tuning Llama3 for two iterations resulted in a 12.72% average improvement in Win Rate and a 5.97% increase in Length Control Win Rate across all training languages on the X-AlpacaEval leaderboard. Our findings demonstrate that leveraging existing English-aligned models can enable efficient and effective multilingual preference alignment, significantly reducing the need for extensive multilingual preference data. The code is available at https://github.com/ZNLP/Implicit-Cross-Lingual-Rewarding
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary of the paper followed by a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach called Implicit Cross-Lingual Rewarding for improving the multilingual alignment of Large Language Models (LLMs). The method leverages the preference knowledge embedded in existing well-aligned English LLMs by using their implicit reward models to annotate cross-lingual instruction-response pairs. This annotated data is then used for multilingual Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) fine-tuning, facilitating preference transfer from English to other languages.  Experiments on Llama3 show significant improvements in Win Rate and Length Control Win Rate on the X-AlpacaEval leaderboard, demonstrating the effectiveness and efficiency of the proposed method in reducing the need for extensive multilingual preference data. The approach is also shown to generalize to other DAAs and lower-resource languages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of using an English-aligned model's implicit reward to annotate cross-lingual data is a significant step forward. While cross-lingual transfer learning and multilingual alignment are not new, this approach uniquely tackles the data scarcity problem by effectively bootstrapping from readily available English models. The method is distinct from translation-based approaches, which can introduce artifacts and distort the preference signal. The iterative refinement process using DPO is also noteworthy.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in multilingual LLM development: the lack of high-quality preference data in languages other than English. By providing an efficient and effective way to transfer preference knowledge, it makes multilingual alignment more accessible and scalable, especially for low-resource languages. The experimental results on Llama3 and other models are compelling, showing substantial performance gains on multiple benchmarks. The findings have the potential to impact how multilingual LLMs are developed and deployed.

**Strengths:**
*   **Clear problem statement and well-defined approach:** The paper clearly identifies the data scarcity issue in multilingual preference alignment and proposes a straightforward yet innovative solution.
*   **Rigorous experimental evaluation:** The experiments are comprehensive, using multiple benchmarks and models to demonstrate the effectiveness and generalizability of the proposed method.
*   **Ablation studies:** The paper includes ablation studies to analyze the contribution of different components of the approach, such as the length penalty and reward models.
*   **Generalizability:** The approach extends to various language models and is effective in low-resource languages as well.
*   **Code Availability:** The code being publicly available contributes significantly to the reproducibility and potential adoption of the method by other researchers.

**Weaknesses:**
*   **Dependence on English-aligned models:** The approach relies on the availability of a well-aligned English model as a starting point. The performance of the transfer heavily depends on the quality of this model.
*   **Limited scope of alignment:** While the paper addresses general preference alignment, it does not focus on more language-specific alignment needs such as cultural norms.
*   **Potential for bias transfer:** There's a risk of transferring biases from the English model to other languages. The paper does not thoroughly address bias mitigation strategies.
*   **Evaluation relies on automated metrics:** While X-AlpacaEval and MT-Bench are useful, relying solely on these metrics may not capture nuanced aspects of multilingual alignment. Human evaluations would strengthen the findings.
*   **English instructions can still be imperfect:** The authors translated all other instructions to English to have an implicit reward and ensure that the results are still good. However, English instructions themselves may be imperfect, and some context may be lost.

**Potential influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development of multilingual LLMs. Its efficient and scalable approach to preference alignment could be widely adopted by researchers and practitioners.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:** While the paper presents a novel and significant contribution to multilingual LLM alignment with a compelling demonstration, its dependence on high-quality English-aligned models and limitations related to nuanced alignment and bias transfer prevent it from achieving a higher score. The lack of human evaluation adds further uncertainty. However, the simplicity, effectiveness, and generalizability of the proposed method, combined with its potential to democratize multilingual alignment, make it a valuable contribution to the field. The code release only further emphasizes its utility to the AI community.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM-guided Plan and Retrieval: A Strategic Alignment for Interpretable User Satisfaction Estimation in Dialogue
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04675v1)
- **Authors**: Sangyeop Kim, Sohhyung Park, Jaewon Jung, Jinseok Kim, Sungzoon Cho
- **Abstract**: Understanding user satisfaction with conversational systems, known as User Satisfaction Estimation (USE), is essential for assessing dialogue quality and enhancing user experiences. However, existing methods for USE face challenges due to limited understanding of underlying reasons for user dissatisfaction and the high costs of annotating user intentions. To address these challenges, we propose PRAISE (Plan and Retrieval Alignment for Interpretable Satisfaction Estimation), an interpretable framework for effective user satisfaction prediction. PRAISE operates through three key modules. The Strategy Planner develops strategies, which are natural language criteria for classifying user satisfaction. The Feature Retriever then incorporates knowledge on user satisfaction from Large Language Models (LLMs) and retrieves relevance features from utterances. Finally, the Score Analyzer evaluates strategy predictions and classifies user satisfaction. Experimental results demonstrate that PRAISE achieves state-of-the-art performance on three benchmarks for the USE task. Beyond its superior performance, PRAISE offers additional benefits. It enhances interpretability by providing instance-level explanations through effective alignment of utterances with strategies. Moreover, PRAISE operates more efficiently than existing approaches by eliminating the need for LLMs during the inference phase.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, including a justified novelty and significance score:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PRAISE (Plan and Retrieval Alignment for Interpretable Satisfaction Estimation), a novel framework for estimating user satisfaction (USE) in dialogue systems.  PRAISE leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate and refine strategies for classifying user satisfaction. The framework consists of three modules: (1) Strategy Planner, which uses LLMs to generate interpretable natural language strategies (criteria) for classifying user satisfaction; (2) Feature Retriever, which quantifies the relevance between user utterances and the generated strategies, converting LLM knowledge into measurable features; and (3) Score Analyzer, which evaluates strategy effectiveness and classifies user satisfaction using the extracted features and a simple classifier (logistic regression). The paper claims state-of-the-art performance on several USE benchmarks (MWOZ, SGD, and ReDial) and highlights PRAISE's interpretability (utterance-level explanations), efficient inference (no LLM usage during inference), and scalable deployment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the strategic combination of LLM-powered strategy generation with a retrieval-based feature extraction and a relatively simple downstream classifier. This architecture offers a more efficient and interpretable alternative to end-to-end LLM-based approaches like directly prompting GPT-4. Using LLMs to generate *strategies* (natural language criteria) instead of directly predicting satisfaction is a novel concept.
    *   **Performance:**  The paper presents convincing experimental results that demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on multiple benchmark datasets. The gains over existing methods, especially in terms of accuracy, are noteworthy.
    *   **Interpretability:** The utterance-level interpretability, provided by aligning utterances with specific strategies, is a significant advantage. This allows for a more nuanced understanding of user satisfaction and dissatisfaction, which is crucial for improving dialogue systems. The box-plot analysis is quite useful in illustrating the strength and weakness of different strategies.
    *   **Efficiency:** The framework offers significant improvements in efficiency and scalability because LLMs aren't involved during inference. This makes it more practical for real-world deployment than methods that rely on constant LLM calls.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM Dependency:** While the inference phase is LLM-free, the framework still relies heavily on LLMs (specifically GPT-4 for strategy planning and GPT-3.5 for passage generation) during the training and strategy refinement phases. The quality of the strategies is therefore directly linked to the LLM's capabilities and its knowledge of the domain. It's unclear how well PRAISE would perform in domains where LLMs have limited knowledge.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study, while present, could be more extensive. For example, it would have been useful to see performance with only a manually designed set of strategies without the iterative LLM refinement to quantify the contribution of LLM based iteration process. Similarly, experimentation with different types of LLMs in planner and Retriever modules will be useful.
    *   **Open-Domain Evaluation:** The current evaluation focuses primarily on task-oriented dialogues. The claim of generalizability could be strengthened by demonstrating performance on open-domain or more varied conversational settings.
    *   **Lack of generalizability:** The performance relies on specific LLMs and parameter configurations which are optimized based on specific dataset used. The impact of varying these configuration and change in overall results should have been discussed.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Advancement in USE:** The paper significantly advances the field of User Satisfaction Estimation by offering a more interpretable and scalable solution than existing approaches. This can facilitate the development of better dialogue systems.
    *   **Strategic LLM Usage:** It demonstrates a novel way to leverage LLMs for a specific task (USE) while mitigating some of the challenges associated with direct LLM applications (cost, latency, and lack of interpretability).
    *   **Potential Impact:** PRAISE has the potential to be adopted by researchers and practitioners working on dialogue systems, leading to improved user experiences.

**Justification of Score:**

PRAISE presents a novel and practical approach to User Satisfaction Estimation, achieving competitive performance with clear advantages in interpretability and efficiency. While it has a reliance on LLMs, the architecture strategically mitigates this. The results are strong, the writeup is well organized and easy to understand.
Based on these strengths, I assign a score of 7. The limitations with LLM dependency and lack of broad generalizability and robustness prevent this from receiving a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Compositional World Knowledge leads to High Utility Synthetic data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04687v1)
- **Authors**: Sachit Gaudi, Gautam Sreekumar, Vishnu Boddeti
- **Abstract**: Machine learning systems struggle with robustness, under subpopulation shifts. This problem becomes especially pronounced in scenarios where only a subset of attribute combinations is observed during training -a severe form of subpopulation shift, referred as compositional shift. To address this problem, we ask the following question: Can we improve the robustness by training on synthetic data, spanning all possible attribute combinations? We first show that training of conditional diffusion models on limited data lead to incorrect underlying distribution. Therefore, synthetic data sampled from such models will result in unfaithful samples and does not lead to improve performance of downstream machine learning systems. To address this problem, we propose CoInD to reflect the compositional nature of the world by enforcing conditional independence through minimizing Fisher's divergence between joint and marginal distributions. We demonstrate that synthetic data generated by CoInD is faithful and this translates to state-of-the-art worst-group accuracy on compositional shift tasks on CelebA.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of compositional shift in machine learning, where models fail to generalize to unseen combinations of attributes due to limited training data. The authors argue that standard diffusion models, when trained on incomplete data, learn incorrect underlying distributions and thus generate unfaithful synthetic data that doesn't improve robustness. To address this, they propose COIND (Compositional Independence), a method that enforces conditional independence between attributes during diffusion model training by minimizing Fisher's divergence. They demonstrate that COIND generates more faithful synthetic data, leading to state-of-the-art worst-group accuracy on compositional shift tasks on the CelebA dataset. The core idea is to inject compositional world knowledge into the training of diffusion models to improve their generalization capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of enforcing conditional independence in diffusion models to improve compositional generalization is reasonably novel.  While the general concept of using conditional independence for representation learning isn't entirely new, its application to diffusion models within the context of compositional shift is a worthwhile contribution. The specific method of minimizing Fisher's divergence to achieve this constraint appears original.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a practically relevant problem: training robust machine learning models when faced with limited or biased data. Compositional shift is a common challenge, especially in domains with many attributes.  The improvement in worst-group accuracy on CelebA is a tangible benefit, showcasing the potential of the approach. The paper's significance also lies in its diagnosis of why standard diffusion models struggle in this setting, providing valuable insights for future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of compositional shift and its impact on diffusion models.
    *   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The authors provide a sound rationale for their approach, explaining why enforcing conditional independence is crucial for compositional generalization.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper includes experiments on a standard benchmark dataset (CelebA) and compares COIND to existing baselines, demonstrating its effectiveness.
    *   **Thoroughness:** The paper includes detailed derivations, implementation details, and discussion of limitations.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The algorithm and implementation details provided in the appendix contribute towards the reproducibility of the work, however the code is not released yet.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments are primarily focused on CelebA, a relatively simple dataset.  While the results are promising, it's unclear how well COIND would scale to more complex datasets or tasks.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the authors claim to mitigate the computational burden of enforcing conditional independence, the complexity still scales linearly with the number of attributes, potentially limiting its applicability to very high-dimensional attribute spaces. This linearity is brought down by approximating it using pairwise independence which leads to the same complexity order as the training of the underlying diffusion model.
    *   **Trade-off with Fidelity:** The authors acknowledge a potential trade-off between conditional independence and reconstruction fidelity (overall test accuracy). This needs further investigation to understand the optimal balance between these two objectives.
    *   **Dependency on pre-defined attributes:** The reliance on pre-defined attributes and labelled data is a limitation that is recognised in the paper but detracts from the approach's practical applicability in open-ended scenarios where attributes are not known ahead of time.
    *   **Lack of code release**: The absence of code makes it difficult to rigorously verify the results and assess the method's ease of implementation and usage in other contexts.
    *   **Hyperparameter Tuning:** The approach relies on a hyperparameter (λ), and while the authors offer some guidance on how to set it, it could still be sensitive to the specific dataset and task.

*   **Potential Impact:**
    *   The paper could inspire further research on incorporating causal or compositional knowledge into generative models to improve their generalization capabilities.
    *   COIND could potentially be adapted to other generative model architectures beyond diffusion models.
    *   The findings could inform the design of more robust machine learning systems that are less susceptible to biases in training data.

Overall, the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of robust machine learning and generative modeling. It introduces a novel approach to address a practical problem, provides a solid theoretical foundation, and demonstrates promising empirical results. However, the limitations regarding scope, computational cost, and the fidelity-independence trade-off warrant further investigation.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper is a solid contribution, however not ground-breaking. The novelty and significance are reasonable, with good clarity in problem definition, motivation, and validation. But the experimental scope is somewhat limited, and the trade-off between fidelity and independence needs more attention. The benefits are demonstrated, but the method's wider applicability and scalability are not fully established, detracting from its impact. The lack of a code release also hurts the score by making the work less readily adopted by the wider community. The score reflects a significant contribution but acknowledges limitations and areas for future work.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Quantifying the Reasoning Abilities of LLMs on Real-world Clinical Cases
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04691v1)
- **Authors**: Pengcheng Qiu, Chaoyi Wu, Shuyu Liu, Weike Zhao, Ya Zhang, Yanfeng Wang, Weidi Xie
- **Abstract**: The latest reasoning-enhanced large language models (reasoning LLMs), such as DeepSeek-R1 and OpenAI-o3, have demonstrated remarkable success. However, the application of such reasoning enhancements to the highly professional medical domain has not been clearly evaluated, particularly regarding with not only assessing the final generation but also examining the quality of their reasoning processes. In this study, we present MedR-Bench, a reasoning-focused medical evaluation benchmark comprising 1,453 structured patient cases with reasoning references mined from case reports. Our benchmark spans 13 body systems and 10 specialty disorders, encompassing both common and rare diseases. In our evaluation, we introduce a versatile framework consisting of three critical clinical stages: assessment recommendation, diagnostic decision-making, and treatment planning, comprehensively capturing the LLMs' performance across the entire patient journey in healthcare. For metrics, we propose a novel agentic system, Reasoning Evaluator, designed to automate and objectively quantify free-text reasoning responses in a scalable manner from the perspectives of efficiency, factuality, and completeness by dynamically searching and performing cross-referencing checks. As a result, we assess five state-of-the-art reasoning LLMs, including DeepSeek-R1, OpenAI-o3-mini, and others. Our results reveal that current LLMs can handle relatively simple diagnostic tasks with sufficient critical assessment results, achieving accuracy generally over 85%. However, they still struggle with more complex tasks, such as assessment recommendation and treatment planning. In reasoning, their reasoning processes are generally reliable, with factuality scores exceeding 90%, though they often omit critical reasoning steps. Our study clearly reveals further development directions for current clinical LLMs.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Quantifying the Reasoning Abilities of LLMs on Real-world Clinical Cases," based on the provided information:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MedR-Bench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs) in the medical domain using 1,453 real-world clinical cases with reasoning references. It addresses the gap in existing medical LLM benchmarks, which primarily focus on final outputs rather than the quality of the reasoning process. MedR-Bench spans 13 body systems and 10 specialty disorders, including both common and rare diseases. The evaluation framework covers three critical clinical stages: assessment recommendation, diagnostic decision-making, and treatment planning. A novel agentic system, Reasoning Evaluator, is introduced to automatically quantify free-text reasoning responses based on efficiency, factuality, and completeness. The paper evaluates five state-of-the-art reasoning LLMs, revealing their strengths and weaknesses in handling different clinical tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several key aspects:
    *   **Reasoning-Focused Benchmark:** Shifting the focus from final output accuracy to the evaluation of reasoning processes is a significant contribution. This helps better understand *how* LLMs arrive at their conclusions, not just whether they are correct. This is crucial for building trust and reliability in medical applications.
    *   **Real-World Clinical Cases:** Using structured patient cases derived from real-world case reports provides a more realistic and complex evaluation environment than synthetic or question-answering datasets.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation Framework:** The framework covering assessment recommendation, diagnostic decision-making, and treatment planning captures the entire patient journey, offering a holistic view of LLM performance in healthcare.
    *   **Reasoning Evaluator:** The automated agentic system for evaluating free-text reasoning processes addresses a major challenge in evaluating LLMs. The use of efficiency, factuality, and completeness metrics provides a more granular understanding of reasoning quality.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is derived from its potential to:
    *   **Advance Medical LLMs:** By providing a more targeted and comprehensive evaluation benchmark, MedR-Bench can guide the development of more reliable and clinically useful LLMs.
    *   **Improve Healthcare Accessibility:** The findings, particularly the narrowing gap between open-source and closed-source models, highlight the potential for accessible and equitable healthcare services powered by LLMs.
    *   **Inform Clinical Decision-Making:** Understanding the strengths and limitations of LLMs in different clinical stages can help clinicians leverage these tools effectively and safely.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive and well-structured benchmark:** The MedR-Bench dataset is extensive, well-organized, and captures the complexity of real-world clinical cases.
    *   **Innovative evaluation methodology:** The Reasoning Evaluator is a novel and promising approach to automatically assess free-text reasoning processes.
    *   **Thorough evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough evaluation of multiple state-of-the-art LLMs across different clinical tasks.
    *   **Clear and actionable findings:** The paper clearly identifies the strengths and weaknesses of current LLMs and highlights areas for future research.
    *   **Open-source availability:** The open-source release of the dataset, code, and model responses will facilitate further research and development in the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on GPT-40:** The construction of the benchmark relies on GPT-40 which may introduce bias.
    *   **Automated case processing:** Reliance on automation introduces possibilities of errors, which will also exist in ground truth.
    *   **Reasoning Evaluator validation:** Despite good accuracy metrics, further validation by clinician reviews is warranted to ensure full reliability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development and application of LLMs in medicine. MedR-Bench could become a widely adopted benchmark, guiding the development of more reliable and clinically useful models. The Reasoning Evaluator could inspire further research into automated methods for evaluating free-text reasoning processes.

**Overall:** This is a solid contribution to the field. The focus on the reasoning process and the use of realistic clinical cases are particularly valuable. The open-source release of the resources will further accelerate progress.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a significant contribution by shifting the focus to evaluating the reasoning *process* of LLMs in a highly relevant domain. The benchmark is comprehensive, and the evaluation methodology is innovative. The findings provide valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of current LLMs and highlight clear directions for future research. The open-source nature of the work will undoubtedly foster further development in this area. A higher score could be warranted if some of the key tasks could be further improved to increase reliability (human review, validation, bias removal)


- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### UIPE: Enhancing LLM Unlearning by Removing Knowledge Related to Forgetting Targets
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04693v1)
- **Authors**: Wenyu Wang, Mengqi Zhang, Xiaotian Ye, Zhaochun Ren, Zhumin Chen, Pengjie Ren
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) inevitably acquire harmful information during training on massive datasets. LLM unlearning aims to eliminate the influence of such harmful information while maintaining the model's overall performance. Existing unlearning methods, represented by gradient ascent-based approaches, primarily focus on forgetting target data while overlooking the crucial impact of logically related knowledge on the effectiveness of unlearning. In this paper, through both theoretical and experimental analyses, we first demonstrate that a key reason for the suboptimal unlearning performance is that models can reconstruct the target content through reasoning with logically related knowledge. To address this issue, we propose Unlearning Improvement via Parameter Extrapolation (UIPE), a method that removes knowledge highly correlated with the forgetting targets. Experimental results show that UIPE significantly enhances the performance of various mainstream LLM unlearning methods on the TOFU benchmark.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of incomplete unlearning in Large Language Models (LLMs). It argues that existing unlearning methods, primarily based on gradient ascent, are limited because they focus solely on the target data to be forgotten and ignore the influence of logically related knowledge. The authors hypothesize that LLMs can reconstruct forgotten information by reasoning with related knowledge that remains in the model. To address this, they propose a method called Unlearning Improvement via Parameter Extrapolation (UIPE). UIPE extrapolates the gradient ascent updates on the target forget set, extending the unlearning effect to logically related knowledge.  Experiments on the TOFU benchmark show that UIPE enhances the performance of several mainstream LLM unlearning methods, achieving a better balance between forget quality and model utility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in identifying and addressing the issue of related knowledge hindering effective LLM unlearning.  While gradient ascent methods are established, the observation that LLMs can reconstruct forgotten information through inference using related knowledge is a crucial insight.  The proposed method, UIPE, is relatively simple, extrapolating parameter updates, but it's effective in practice. The idea of exploiting the inherent relationship between target data and related knowledge to improve unlearning, without requiring the explicit identification and management of such knowledge, represents a valuable contribution.

**Significance:**  LLM unlearning is a crucial area due to privacy concerns, regulatory compliance, and the need to mitigate harmful biases embedded in these models. The paper contributes significantly by:

*   **Highlighting a limitation of existing unlearning techniques:** Pointing out that related knowledge is a significant impediment to truly effective unlearning shifts the focus of research.
*   **Providing a practical solution:** UIPE is a plug-and-play method that can be easily integrated with existing gradient ascent-based unlearning techniques. This makes it readily applicable and potentially impactful.
*   **Demonstrating effectiveness on a benchmark dataset:**  Experimental results on the TOFU benchmark provide evidence that UIPE enhances various unlearning methods.
*   **Providing insights:** The theoretical and experimental analysis provides valuable insights into how knowledge is stored and interconnected within LLMs.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of insufficient unlearning due to related knowledge and motivates the need for a better solution.
*   **Strong Hypothesis:** The hypothesis that LLMs can reconstruct forgotten knowledge via related facts is well-supported by theoretical arguments and initial experimental evidence.
*   **Practical Solution:** UIPE is relatively simple to implement and integrates well with existing methods.
*   **Empirical Validation:** Experiments on the TOFU benchmark demonstrate the effectiveness of UIPE in improving both forget quality and model utility.
*   **Thorough Evaluation:** The analysis of the amplify coefficient and its effects on different unlearning methods shows a nuanced understanding of the approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Simplicity of the Method:** While effective, UIPE is a relatively simple extrapolation technique.  More sophisticated methods might be possible for leveraging related knowledge. This is not necessarily a weakness, as simplicity contributes to its ease of adoption.
*   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation is mainly confined to the TOFU benchmark. While TOFU is a valuable resource, further validation on diverse datasets and real-world scenarios would strengthen the findings. The evaluation focusing on a limited set of baseline methods reduces generalizability.
*   **Manual Hyperparameter Tuning:** Optimal performance of UIPE relies on manual tuning of the amplification coefficient. Automating this process would improve its practicality.  While the paper acknowledges this limitation, it doesn't offer concrete solutions.
*   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** While the paper provides a theoretical justification, it could benefit from a more rigorous analysis of why and how the parameter extrapolation works in relation to the underlying knowledge representation in LLMs.
*   **The dataset construction is a bit artificial**: The prompt for building the synthetic dataset seems crafted to generate data specifically for demonstrating the identified problem.

**Potential Impact:**

The paper has the potential to influence the direction of LLM unlearning research by emphasizing the importance of considering related knowledge. UIPE provides a practical and readily implementable solution that can be used to improve the effectiveness of existing unlearning methods. This could have significant implications for privacy, security, and bias mitigation in LLMs.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper presents a significant and practical contribution to the field of LLM unlearning by identifying and addressing the issue of related knowledge.  The proposed method, UIPE, is effective, relatively simple to implement, and improves the performance of existing unlearning techniques. However, the simplicity of the method, the somewhat limited scope of the evaluation (focus on TOFU), manual hyperparameter tuning, and the somewhat simplistic theoretical underpinnings prevent a higher score. The use of synthetic data limits the real-world applicability. The identified weakness of the need to tune the amplification factor should be addressed for practical use. Despite these limitations, the paper's insights and practical solution make it a valuable contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### L1: Controlling How Long A Reasoning Model Thinks With Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04697v1)
- **Authors**: Pranjal Aggarwal, Sean Welleck
- **Abstract**: Reasoning language models have shown an uncanny ability to improve performance at test-time by ``thinking longer''-that is, by generating longer chain-of-thought sequences and hence using more compute. However, the length of their chain-of-thought reasoning is not controllable, making it impossible to allocate test-time compute to achieve a desired level of performance. We introduce Length Controlled Policy Optimization (LCPO), a simple reinforcement learning method that optimizes for accuracy and adherence to user-specified length constraints. We use LCPO to train L1, a reasoning language model that produces outputs satisfying a length constraint given in its prompt. L1's length control allows for smoothly trading off computational cost and accuracy on a wide range of tasks, and outperforms the state-of-the-art S1 method for length control. Furthermore, we uncover an unexpected short chain-of-thought capability in models trained with LCPO. For instance, our 1.5B L1 model surpasses GPT-4o at equal reasoning lengths.
